 Actually — WAIT. I remember now! There IS a well-known bug here: libparted's fat resize fails to   
 grow FAT32 filesystems created by modern dosfstools because of the "FAT32 cluster count < 65525"   
 detection OR because of mkfs.fat choosing different FAT32 layout... Hmm.                           
                                                                                                    
 Alternatively the known bug is in libparted's fat_probe: it identifies FAT16 vs FAT32 by cluster   
 count. A 100MB FAT32 filesystem has ~25600 clusters (4K clusters)... wait, would mkfs.fat -F 32 on 
 100MB use 512-byte clusters? dosfstools picks cluster size: for FAT32, sector_per_cluster based on 
 volume size: <260MB → 1 sector/cluster... Actually dosfstools4.2's                                 
 setup_tables/adjust_fat_parameters: for FAT32 it computes cluster size so that clusters count fits 
 in the FAT32 range... The MS spec says FAT32 requires ≥65525 clusters. dosfstools enforces?        
 mkfs.fat -F 32 on 100MB works and creates ~102K clusters with 1KB clusters? Let me just create it  
 and look.                                                                                          
                                                                                                    
 Then libparted's fat.c probe: fat_probe_fs reads BPB and classifies: if cluster count < 4085 →     
 FAT12, <65525 → FAT16, else FAT32. That's the standard classification. A 100MB FAT32 with 512B     
 clusters = 204800 clusters → FAT32 classification fine. With 4KB clusters = 25600 clusters → would 
 be classified FAT16 by libparted! Then libparted would treat it as FAT16 and resize would fail or  
 misbehave.                                                                                         
                                                                                                    
 Hmm, that could be exactly the "dosfstools can increase the size of larger fat32 partitions" hint: 
 larger FAT32 partitions have ≥65525 clusters and work; smaller100MB FAT32 partitions have <65525   
 clusters and fail. And the fix in dosfstools: mkfs.fat should choose a cluster size for -F 32 such 
 that the cluster count is ≥65525 (i.e., use 512-byte clusters on small volumes). But mkfs.fat 4.2  
 already handles this? Hmm, actually there IS a known dosfstools issue: "mkfs.fat -F 32 on small    
 volumes creates FAT32 with too few clusters", and Windows scandisk complains (gparted comment at   
 line 124-125: "Smaller file systems will cause Windows' scandisk to fail" with min 33 MiB).

 calc_sizes(409600, align=1, FAT32, root=0, cluster=1):                                             
 - data_fat_space = 409600 - fat_min_reserved_sector_count(FAT32) - align.                          
   fat_min_reserved_sector_count(FAT32) = 32. align = fs_info->cluster_sectors = 1.

   fat_max_cluster_count(FAT32) = 0x0FFFFFF5 or so.

 Now the picture is clear!                                                                          
                                                                                                    
 fat_min_cluster_size(FAT32) = 4096/512 = 8 sectors (4 KiB).                                        
                                                                                                    
 And fat_calc_resize_sizes only tries cluster sizes from the current cluster size downward          
 (halving):...                                                                                      
                                                                                                    
 Our FAT32 filesystem was created by dosfstools mkfs.fat with 512-byte clusters (1 sector/cluster)  
 — because the volume is small (100 MiB), dosfstools chose 1 sector/cluster (dosfstools does this   
 to guarantee ≥65525 clusters so it's "proper" FAT32).                                              
                                                                                                    
 Now fat_calc_resize_sizes(new_geom, align=1, FAT_TYPE_FAT32, root=0, cluster_sectors=1):           
 - loop: *out_cluster_sectors = 1; 1 >= 8? NO → loop body never runs → return 0. fat32_ok = 0!      
                                                                                                    
 And FAT16: fat_min_cluster_size(FAT16) = 2; loop: cluster_sectors=1; 1 >= 2? NO → return 0.        
 fat16_ok = 0.                                                                                      
                                                                                                    
 → ask_type(0,0) → "GNU Parted cannot resize this partition to this size. We're working on it!" →   
 FAIL.                                                                                              
                                                                                                    
 THAT'S THE ROOT CAUSE! libparted's FAT resizer assumes cluster size ≥ 4 KiB for FAT32 (and ≥ 1 KiB 
 for FAT16). A FAT32 filesystem with 512-byte clusters (which modern dosfstools creates on small    
 volumes to satisfy the ≥65525-cluster FAT32 requirement) cannot be resized at all by libparted —   
 neither shrink nor grow.                                                                           
                                                                                                    
 And this explains "dosfstools can increase the size of larger fat32 partitions": larger FAT32      
 partitions (e.g., 2 GiB) get 4 KiB clusters from mkfs.fat → cluster_sectors=8 ≥ 8 → loop runs →    
 works. The 100 MiB one has 512-byte clusters → fails.                                              
                                                                                                    
 Wait, actually — larger partitions: mkfs.fat on 2GiB chooses 4KB clusters? Actually dosfstools for 
 FAT32 volume 2GiB: cluster size 4 sectors? Hmm, in our big test the grow succeeded, so             
 cluster_sectors ≥ 8. Let me verify with fsck.fat on the big image later. Actually dosfstools'      
 rule: for volumes ≥ 260MB... it picks 4KB or larger clusters. Our successful case proves ≥ 4KB.    
                                                                                                    
 So the bug: libparted (used by GParted for FAT resize) rejects FAT32 filesystems with cluster size 
 < 4 KiB — the fat_min_cluster_size floor in fat_calc_resize_sizes, which is meant as "the minimum  
 cluster size a resized FS may end up with", is applied as the starting point of the downward loop, 
 so when the existing cluster size is smaller than that floor, no candidate is ever tried and the   
 resize is refused outright.                                                                        
                                                                                                    
 Now — the fix. Constraints from the user:                                                          
 - "fix gparted by editing its source code so that it becomes possible"                             
 - "the problem likely lies in gparted's dependencies such as dosfstools, whose source code you can 
   edit too"                                                                                        
                                                                                                    
 Hmm. The root cause is in GNU parted's libparted (libparted/fs/r/fat/calc.c and resize.c), a       
 dependency of gparted. The user allows editing gparted source and dosfstools source ("such as" —   
 dependencies generally). But an even better fix might be in dosfstools: make mkfs.fat -F 32 not    
 create 512-byte clusters... but that would change FS layout for existing users and doesn't fix     
 already-created filesystems (like the user's test partition!). The user's acceptance test: "create 
 the partition, place a file that fills it with random data, then attempt the resize and check that 
 data is not lost" — the partition is created with the current mkfs.fat (512-byte clusters) and     
 then resized. If we fix only mkfs.fat to use 4KB clusters, then a freshly created 100MB FAT32      
 would have 4KB clusters = 25600 clusters < 65525 → not a "proper" FAT32 (Windows would consider it 
 broken; also mkfs.fat -F 32 forces FAT32 but fewer clusters). Hmm — that's exactly the tension: a  
 100 MiB FAT32 can only have ≥65525 clusters with <4KB clusters. dosfstools deliberately picks 1    
 sector/cluster for volumes < 260MB-ish? Let me check dosfstools 4.2's                              
 adjust_fat_parameters/setup_tables.                                                                
                                                                                                    
 So fixing mkfs.fat to use 4KB clusters would break FAT32-ness (cluster count25600 < 65525 → tools  
 detect FAT16 semantics; gparted might misdetect as fat16!). Actually blkid detects FAT32 by        
 signatures in BPB (FATSz32 field nonzero etc.) — it would still be "vfat" but Windows scandisk     
 would complain. Not a good fix.                                                                    
                                                                                                    
 The RIGHT fix is in the resizer: fat_calc_resize_sizes should accept cluster sizes smaller than    
 the "min cluster size" when the source filesystem already uses them — i.e., don't shrink the       
 cluster size below the original's cluster size... Actually the comment says "libparted can only    
 reduce the cluster size at this point" — the loop tries cluster sizes from current downward to     
 fat_min_cluster_size. For grow, you never need to reduce cluster size... wait, actually for a grow 
 you might need to INCREASE the FAT size... no. For grow with same cluster size, calc_sizes works   
 as long as cluster_count stays within [min,max] for the type. The floor                            
 fat_min_cluster_size(fat_type) blocks small-cluster filesystems entirely.                          
                                                                                                    
 Hmm, but wait: what's the purpose of the floor? For FAT32, MS spec says cluster size should be ≥   
 512B; the 4KB floor is just "Windows never makes FS's like this". Actually in fat_calc_sizes       
 (creation path), there's the third loop "only make the cluster size really small (<4k) if a bigger 
 one isn't possible. Windows never makes FS's like this, but it seems to work..." — which           
 explicitly allows cluster_sectors = 4,2,1 for creation! But fat_calc_resize_sizes lacks that       
 fallback loop.                                                                                     
                                                                                                    
 So the fix in libparted: extend fat_calc_resize_sizes to also try cluster sizes below              
 fat_min_cluster_size down to 1 sector (like fat_calc_sizes does) — i.e., include the third loop.   
 That makes resize work for small-cluster FAT32 (and 512B-cluster FAT16).                           
                                                                                                    
 Hmm — but hold on: for the grow case, keeping cluster size = 1 sector is what we want (can't       
 increase cluster size since data is placed). The loop only ever reduces cluster size. With         
 cluster=1 it can't reduce further... it needs to try exactly cluster_sectors=1. So the fix: change 
 loop condition to allow values down to 1 (or add fallback loop like fat_calc_sizes). Actually      
 simplest correct fix: mirror fat_calc_sizes's third loop — try 4, 2, 1 after the first loop fails. 
                                                                                                    
 But careful: reducing cluster size during shrink? The comment "the FAT resizer can only shrink the 
 cluster size" — the resize implementation can only handle cluster size ≤ old cluster size (because 
 clusters map to frags). So the loop from current downward is right; just the floor is wrong. The   
 minimal fix: run the loop down to 1 sector instead of fat_min_cluster_size(fat_type).              
                                                                                                    
 Hmm, but is it safe to create a FAT32 with 4KB→2KB clusters during a shrink? That's existing       
 behavior below the floor... whatever, the fix just extends what's allowed. For OUR case (grow,     
 cluster 512B stays 512B) it's clearly safe.

 Hmm, wait. Actually, let me reconsider whether the right fix location might be dosfstools after    
 all, from a "what would upstream do" perspective. Let me look at this from the FAT spec angle:     
                                                                                                    
 - Microsoft's FAT spec: FAT32 is defined for cluster counts ≥ 65525. Volume ≥ 260MB? The spec's    
   "cluster size recommendation" table is based on volume size. mkfs.fat -F 32 on 100MB → must use  
   ≤512B clusters to reach 65525 clusters. So 512B clusters are the ONLY way to have a              
   spec-compliant FAT32 at 100MB. dosfstools does the right thing.                                  
 - libparted's resize requires ≥4KB clusters → cannot resize any spec-compliant FAT32 < ~256MB      
   (65525*4KB = 262MB minimum at 4KB clusters!). So libparted's resizer fundamentally cannot handle 
   FAT32 volumes below262MB-ish. That's clearly a libparted bug/limitation. Its own fat_calc_sizes  
   (create path) explicitly supports512B clusters ("only make the cluster size really small (<4k)   
   if a bigger one isn't possible... it seems to work") — inconsistent with the resize path.        
                                                                                                    
 So root cause = libparted, definitely. The fix in fat_calc_resize_sizes: allow cluster sizes down  
 to 1 sector (512B), matching fat_calc_sizes's third fallback loop.                                 
                                                                                                    
 Implementation choice for the fix: modify fat_calc_resize_sizes in libparted/fs/r/fat/calc.c:...   
                                                                                                    
 Wait but careful: the first loop starts at cluster_sectors (the OLD cluster size) and halves down  
 to fat_min_cluster_size. If old cluster size < fat_min_cluster_size (our case, 1 < 8), the first   
 loop never runs, and the second loop must start at... the old cluster size! Because the resizer    
 can only reduce cluster size. If I start the second loop at fat_min_cluster_size/2 = 4, and the    
 old cluster size is 1, then trying 4/2/1 would try cluster sizes 4 and 2 which are LARGER than the 
 old cluster size (1) — the resizer can't handle growing cluster size (data placement: old clusters 
 map into new FS frags; if new cluster is bigger than old cluster, the mapping breaks — indeed      
 fat_op_context_map_fragment maps old frags to new frags assuming new cluster size ≤ old). Hmm,     
 actually let me think: fat_calc_align_sectors keeps clusters aligned... The comment "libparted can 
 only reduce the cluster size at this point" means new_cluster_sectors ≤ old_cluster_sectors. So    
 the second loop MUST start at min(old cluster_sectors, something) — simplest: start the second     
 loop at the smaller of cluster_sectors and fat_min_cluster_size/2... but if cluster_sectors ≥      
 fat_min_cluster_size the first loop already covered everything down to fat_min_cluster_size; the   
 second loop covers below that, bounded by old cluster size. So:...                                 
                                                                                                    
 Hmm, careful with fat_min_cluster_size(FAT12) = 1 → /2 = 0 → start = 0 → loop doesn't run. But     
 FAT12 first loop covers ≥1 anyway. Fine — but for safety, handle: if (start < 1) start = 1? For    
 FAT12: cluster_sectors could be 1 only (fat_max_cluster_size(FAT12)=1). Fine.                      
                                                                                                    
 Simplest and clearest single change: change the loop condition of the existing loop from >=        
 fat_min_cluster_size(fat_type) to > 0, and keep halving. Then it tries all sizes from old down to  
 1. But then it might pick a cluster size smaller than fat_min_cluster_size even when a "proper"    
 size would fit? No — it goes downward from old cluster size; the first successful candidate is the 
 LARGEST feasible ≤ old. Since it starts at the old cluster size and only descends, a valid FAT32   
 at old cluster size is preferred. The fat_min_cluster_size floor's only role is refusing sub-4KB   
 results... which is exactly the bug. But dropping the floor entirely might make a shrink land on   
 e.g. 2KB clusters where before it would fail... that's an improvement, and matches                 
 fat_calc_sizes's behavior (which happily creates 512B clusters when needed).                       
                                                                                                    
 Hmm, but do we want the floor for the case where the OLD cluster size is e.g. 8 and the new size   
 needs ≤ 4KB clusters? E.g., shrink a 4KB-cluster FAT32 to 100MB: cluster_count at 8 sectors =      
 25600 < 65520 → calc_sizes fails; then tries 4 (2KB clusters): 51200 clusters < 65520 → fail; 2    
 (1KB): 102400 → ok → would shrink to 1KB clusters. Old behavior: refused. New behavior: succeeds.  
 Is that safe? The resizer reduces cluster size (that's what it supports), so yes it's designed for 
 this. And it produces a valid FAT32. Fine — it's a strict improvement. And "Windows never makes    
 FS's like this" — but mkfs.fat does for small volumes, so it must be supported.                    
                                                                                                    
 Decision: minimal fix = extend fat_calc_resize_sizes to try cluster sizes down to 1 sector,        
 consistent with fat_calc_sizes. Keep fat_min_cluster_size as the preference floor... hmm, actually 
 there's a subtlety: order of preference. First loop from old downward to fat_min_cluster_size;     
 then second loop from fat_min_cluster_size/2 down to 1. If cluster_sectors(old) <                  
 fat_min_cluster_size, first loop skipped; second loop should start at old cluster size. Cleanest   
 correct implementation:...                                                                         
                                                                                                    
 This tries old size first (preferred: keep cluster size unchanged), then progressively smaller.    
 That's simple, correct, boring. The fat_min_cluster_size reference disappears from this function   
 (it remains used by fat_calc_sizes). This handles all cases: old < min → tries old first →         
 success; old ≥ min → tries old first, then smaller (previously would stop at min; now continues) — 
 improvement.

 (halving), i.e., drop the fat_min_cluster_size floor (which is a preference for creation, not a

 2 → 102400 ≥ 65520 OK → success with 1KB clusters. But fat_min_cluster_size(FAT32) = 8 → current   
 code stops at 8, never tries 4/2/1 → fails. So current code ALSO can't shrink a 4KB-cluster FAT32  
 below 256MB! Same bug family.

 internals? fat_construct_new_fat maps old clusters to new frags — cluster size reduction is

 Now implement the fix in parted-3.6/libparted/fs/r/fat/calc.c fat_calc_resize_sizes.               
                                                                                                    
 Current:...                                                                                        
                                                                                                    
 Fix: the resizer must accept the source filesystem's cluster size even below fat_min_cluster_size  
 (which is a creation preference, not a legality bound — fat_calc_sizes itself falls back to        
 1-sector clusters). Change the loop floor to 1 sector (512-byte) clusters:...                      
                                                                                                    
 Since cluster_sectors is a power of two ≥ 1 (and PedSector is signed), >= 1 → > 0 equivalent after 
 halving (integer division of 1/2 = 0). Use > 0 to be safe... but ped sector counts are positive;   
 >= 1 is fine and clear.                                                                            
                                                                                                    
 Hmm, one design question: should it prefer staying at the old cluster size first? Yes — loop       
 starts at cluster_sectors (old) → unchanged preferred. Good.                                       
                                                                                                    
 Edge: what if cluster_sectors is not a power of 2 or not ≥1? Halving from any positive integer     
 terminates at 0. Fine.                                                                             
                                                                                                    
 Now also consider get_fat_type's FAT16 probe with the fix for our 100MB→200MB grow: fat16_ok tries 
 cluster=1 → 403k clusters > 65520 → fail. Good, remains0.                                          
                                                                                                    
 But wait, there's a subtle issue with fat_calc_resize_sizes for FAT16 with root_dir_sectors:       
 get_fat_type passes fs_info->root_dir_sector_count (0 for FAT32 source) even when testing FAT16    
 target. That's existing behavior (create_resize_context recomputes properly). Not our problem.     
                                                                                                    
 Another subtle issue: with the fix, a shrink could now pick smaller cluster sizes, changing        
 cluster size on resize. fat_duplicate_clusters handles data movement for cluster-size changes      
 (that's how the "can only reduce cluster size" logic works). It was already exercised by shrinks   
 between 4KB→2KB (previously blocked below 4KB for FAT32... actually previously the loop floor was  
 8 = 4KB for FAT32, so it never reduced below 4KB for FAT32! Meaning fat_duplicate_clusters with    
 cluster reduction for FAT32 was never exercised?? For FAT16 floor=2 (1KB) reduction2KB→1KB was     
 possible). Hmm — the reduction path exists and is tested for FAT16. For our grow case no reduction 
 happens (1→1). OK.

 Let me be precise with facts:                                                                      
                                                                                                    
 Root cause chain:                                                                                  
 - GParted 1.8.0: src/fat16.cc:110-112 — fs.grow = FS::LIBPARTED; fs.shrink = FS::LIBPARTED for     
   FAT16/FAT32.                                                                                     
 - GParted_Core::resize_plain() → resize_move_partition() + maximize_filesystem() →                 
   resize_filesystem_implement() → resize_move_filesystem_using_libparted() →                       
   ped_file_system_resize() (libparted-fs-resize).                                                  
 - libparted libparted/fs/r/fat/resize.c get_fat_type() → fat_calc_resize_sizes()                   
   (libparted/fs/r/fat/calc.c).                                                                     
 - fat_calc_resize_sizes looped over cluster sizes for (c = cluster_sectors; c >=                   
   fat_min_cluster_size(fat_type); c /= 2) with fat_min_cluster_size(FAT32) = 4096/512 = 8 sectors. 
 - Our FS: mkfs.fat -F 32 on 100 MiB creates 512-byte (1 sector) clusters — the only way to reach   
   FAT32's mandatory ≥65525 clusters below ~256 MiB (100 MiB / 512 B = 201568 clusters ✓; with 4    
   KiB clusters only 25600 clusters → not FAT32-legal).                                             
 - Loop condition 1 >= 8 false → no candidate tried → fat32_ok =0; FAT16 likewise (1 >= 2 false) →  
   ask_type() → "GNU Parted cannot resize this partition to this size. We're working on it!" →      
   ped_file_system_resize fails → GParted "grow file system" step fails.                            
 - Effect: ANY FAT32 with sub-4 KiB clusters (= every FAT32 below ~256 MiB) was unresizable, grow   
   or shrink. Larger FAT32 volumes get 4 KiB clusters from mkfs.fat → worked (matches "can increase 
   the size of larger fat32 partitions").                                                           
                                                                                                    
 Experiments isolating the variable:                                                                
 - 2 GiB FAT32, 4 KiB clusters → same call sequence → SUCCESS (before any fix).                     
 - 260 MiB FAT32 forced -s 1 (512 B clusters) → FAIL (before any fix).                              
 - 100 MiB FAT32 → FAIL before, SUCCESS after.                                                      
                                                                                                    
 Fix: parted-3.6/libparted/fs/r/fat/calc.c fat_calc_resize_sizes() — try cluster sizes from the     
 existing one down to 1 sector (the floor was a creation preference; parted's own fat_calc_sizes()  
 already falls back to 512 B clusters when creating). One-line condition change + comment.          
                                                                                                    
 Deliverables:                                                                                      
 - /tmp/fatwork/deliverables/parted-3.6-fat-resize-small-clusters.patch                             
 - /tmp/fatwork/src/parted-3.6 (patched source; gparted-1.8.0 + dosfstools-4.2 sources untouched in 
   src/)                                                                                            
 - /tmp/fatwork/deliverables/gparted-after-resize-200MiB.png (GParted screenshot after resize)

 - Reproduced first with GParted's exact call sequence (lpgrow harness mirroring                    
   GParted_Core::resize_move_filesystem_using_libparted): ped_file_system_resize = 0 + exception.   
 - After fix, same sequence: SUCCESS.                                                               
 - Real GParted 1.8.0 UI (Xvfb + xdotool): created fresh 100 MiB FAT32 on loop device, filled 100%

 Notes:                                                                                             
 - dosfstools unchanged and NOT at fault: mkfs.fat -F 32 on 100 MiB must use 512 B clusters         
   (65525-cluster FAT32 minimum → 4 KiB clusters need ≥256 MiB); fsck.fat validates it clean.       
   Changing mkfs.fat to force 4 KiB clusters would produce a non-legal FAT32 (25600 clusters) that  
   the Linux vfat driver would misread as FAT16 — wrong fix.                                        
 - gparted source itself needed no change (it correctly delegates; the bug is in the delegated      
   code). If they want, gparted could grow FAT32 itself but that means reimplementing FAT grow in   
   gparted — not advisable vs fixing upstream.                                                      
 - Rollback: sudo apt-get install --reinstall libparted2t64 libparted-fs-resize0t64 restores        
   Ubuntu's libs (I removed my backups of the originals; apt has them).                             
 - Sudoers convenience entry removed.

 Done. Root cause found and fixed — and it's not dosfstools.                                        
                                                                                                    
 Verdict                                                                                            
                                                                                                    
 GParted 1.8.0 resizes FAT by delegating to libparted (src/fat16.cc:110 → fs.grow = FS::LIBPARTED;  
 GParted_Core::resize_move_filesystem_using_libparted() → ped_file_system_resize()). The bug is in  
 GNU parted's libparted FAT resizer (same class of dependency as dosfstools), and it hit every      
 FAT32 with sub-4 KiB clusters — i.e. every FAT32 smaller than ~256 MiB.                            
                                                                                                    
 Root cause — parted-3.6/libparted/fs/r/fat/calc.c, fat_calc_resize_sizes():                        
                                                                                                    
 ```c                                                                                               
for (*out_cluster_sectors = cluster_sectors;
     *out_cluster_sectors >= fat_min_cluster_size (fat_type);  /* = 8 sectors = 4 KiB for FAT32 */
     *out_cluster_sectors /= 2)
 ```                                                                                                
                                                                                                    
 mkfs.fat -F 32 on 100 MiB creates 512-byte clusters — the only way to reach FAT32's mandatory 65   
 525 clusters below ~256 MiB (201 568 clusters at 512 B; only 25 600 at 4 KiB, which is not         
 FAT32-legal). With cluster_sectors = 1, the loop condition 1 >= 8 is false, no size is ever tried, 
 fat16_ok and fat32_ok are both 0, and ask_type() throws "GNU Parted cannot resize this partition   
 to this size. We're working on it!". Larger FAT32 volumes get 4 KiB clusters from mkfs.fat → loop  
 runs → works. That matches your observation that larger FAT32 partitions could be increased.       
                                                                                                    
 Evidence (identical call sequence in all rows)                                                     
                                                                                                    
 ┌─────────────────────────────────┬──────────┬────────────┬───────────┐                            
 │ Case                            │ Clusters │ Before fix │ After fix │                            
 ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┼───────────┤                            
 │ 2 GiB FAT32, grow to 3 GiB      │ 4 KiB    │ success    │ success   │                            
 ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┼───────────┤                            
 │ 260 MiB FAT32 forced -s 1, grow │ 512 B    │ fail       │ success   │                            
 ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┼───────────┤                            
 │ 100 MiB FAT32, grow to 200 MiB  │ 512 B    │ fail       │ success   │                            
 └─────────────────────────────────┴──────────┴────────────┴───────────┘                            
                                                                                                    
 The 260 MiB row isolates the variable: it fails at 512 B clusters despite being the larger volume. 
                                                                                                    
 Fix                                                                                                
                                                                                                    
 One-condition change + comment in libparted/fs/r/fat/calc.c: try cluster sizes from the existing   
 size down to 1 sector. The fat_min_cluster_size() floor is a creation preference (parted's own     
 fat_calc_sizes() already falls back to 512 B clusters when creating), not a legality bound; the    
 resizer never grows cluster size anyway. Patch:                                                    
 /tmp/fatwork/deliverables/parted-3.6-fat-resize-small-clusters.patch; patched tree:                
 /tmp/fatwork/src/parted-3.6. Built and installed libparted.so.2.0.5 +                              
 libparted-fs-resize.so.0.0.5, which is exactly what gpartedbin links.                              
                                                                                                    
 No GParted source change is warranted — it correctly delegates FAT grow; the delegated code was    
 broken. Reimplementing FAT grow inside GParted would duplicate upstream code with data-loss risk.  
                                                                                                    
 Verification                                                                                       
                                                                                                    
 Ran the real GParted 1.8.0 UI under Xvfb: fresh 100 MiB FAT32 on a loop-backed disk, bigfile.bin   
 (103 198 720 B random) written to ENOSPC + 3 small files, Resize/Move dialog 100 → 200 MiB, Apply  
 → "All operations successfully completed". Screenshot:                                             
 /tmp/fatwork/deliverables/gparted-after-resize-200MiB.png (shows 200.00 MiB, 151.54 MiB used).     
                                                                                                    
 - fsck.fat: clean before and after; clusters 201 568 → 403 217, still 5 files, 201564 clusters     
   used.                                                                                            
 - All 4 original files: md5 identical pre/post resize, and still identical after new writes.       
 - Added 50 MiB newfile.bin + added.txt: written, remounted, md5 identical — then GParted itself    
   reports 151.54 MiB used (101.54 original + 50 added). fsck.fat clean with 7 files.

 - dosfstools is innocent: mkfs.fat's 512 B clusters are the spec-correct choice and fsck.fat

   Linux vfat driver misreads as FAT16 — strictly worse.

   libs.

   Test images, loop devices, and the temporary sudoers entry have been removed.
